Tom Lee-chaired Ethereum Ethereum (ETH) treasury company Bitmine's Bitmine Immersion Technologies, Inc. (BMNR) holdings have reached 6,070,092 ETH, worth over $12.9 billion at current prices after its latest weekly acquisitions.

Bitmine bought 9,587 ETH last week, reporting on Monday that its total crypto, cash, marketable securities, and other investments stood at $14.3 billion. While Bitmine did not disclose an average purchase price, its latest acquisition is worth around $20.3 million at current prices.

"Vitalik Buterin, founder of Ethereum, declared at Devcon 8 in Mumbai, 'I've finally made up my mind, ETH is a store of value, ETH is money,'" Lee said in the statement. "This declaration by the founder of Ethereum, in my view, is a seismic shift, clarifying how ETH will capture value in this coming supercycle of tokenization and AI."

"In my opinion, ETH was not a 'cash flow' token model, like HYPE or LIT, but rather pristine and trusted collateral, and reflective of how Ethereum is the Layer 1 chosen by the vast majority of financial institutions moving assets onto the blockchain," Lee added.

As of Nov. 8, Bitmine also holds 216 BTC ($16.1 million), a $75 million stake in Eightco Holdings, a $180 million stake in Beast Industries, and total cash and marketable securities of $475 million, the firm said.

Bitmine's ETH holdings now represent around 4.97% of Ethereum's current circulating supply, which sits at approximately 122.2 million ETH, per The Block's ether price page. The firm is now 99% of the way toward its "Alchemy of 5%" supply target since embarking on its Ethereum treasury strategy in June 2025.

As of Nov. 9, Bitmine said its total staked Ethereum stands at 5,227,037 ETH ($11.1 billion), or more than 86% of its total haul. Once its ether holdings are fully staked, the firm projects $359 million in annualized staking rewards, compared to $309 million in annualized rewards at current staking levels, Lee said.

Bitmine remains the largest Ethereum treasury holder and the second-largest public crypto treasury company overall, behind Michael Saylor's Strategy, which holds 850,364 BTC ($63.4 billion) following its latest weekly acquisition.

Considering there is no such Meta VR Plus service currently, the obvious conclusion is that Meta Horizon+ is being renamed once again.

Meta Horizon+ currently includes over 100 games with its $7.99 per month subscription and is similar to Microsoft’s Xbox Game Pass service in many ways. Jensen Huang Hired a PR Firm Known for Handling Sex Scandals Last Year

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang began working with a crisis communications firm known for handling public scandals a year before the CEO admitted to having an extramarital affair with a woman suspected by US intelligence of being a Chinese spy. The firm, Heller, has handled Huang’s behind-the-scenes communications since at least October 2025, according to two sources familiar with the matter.

Risa Heller, the firm’s founder, has built a reputation as a fixer for powerful men facing allegations of sexual misconduct and other personal controversies, according to a 2023 New York Magazine profile. Her past clients and people who have sought her advice include former congressman Anthony Weiner, chef Mario Batali, and writer Jeffrey Toobin. Heller also handles corporate communications and issues unrelated to personal controversies for its clients.

Heller’s public relations consultants were initially believed to be hired for matters related to Huang’s personal life, including his family’s philanthropic endeavors, according to people familiar with the arrangement. The firm frequently communicated with Huang and his daughter Madison, a product marketing director at Nvidia who has become an increasingly prominent figure within the company, sources tell WIRED. It’s not clear whether Heller’s early work with the Huang family involved Huang’s affair.

In 2024, Huang began appearing in public with a woman who goes by Alice Huang, a former executive at the Singapore-based chip buyer Megaspeed, according to The New York Times. Megaspeed was created when it split off from a Chinese gaming and cloud computing firm in 2023, a few months after the US government began restricting exports of advanced semiconductors to China, including those developed by Nvidia. A subsidiary of Megaspeed later became a major buyer of Nvidia chips.

In early October 2025, Jensen Huang’s business dealings with Megaspeed and his connection to Alice became public in a lengthy New York Times report, which alleged that the US Commerce Department was investigating whether the company was helping Chinese firms evade American export controls through its subsidiaries.

The report caused tensions to rise within Nvidia’s top ranks, and Jensen was rankled by the story, according to sources. By the end of that month, Nvidia’s internal communications teams had been instructed to include Heller’s firm on any press inquiries about Huang, whether personal or professional, according to people familiar with the matter.

When reached by email, a representative for Heller referred WIRED to Nvidia. Nvidia did not immediately respond to requests for comment.

US intelligence officials have determined that Alice (who has no known relation to Jensen) is a spy working for the Chinese government, according to the Times. Nvidia initially said that Jensen’s interactions with Alice were part of routine business with clients. But on Sunday, Huang acknowledged that his relationship with her was romantic in nature. In an email to Nvidia staff, he said he owed them and his family an apology. “I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry,” Huang wrote.

Nvidia’s board of directors announced it was opening an independent investigation into Jensen’s activities. It said it had no reason to believe that national security or confidential Nvidia information was compromised.

Before founding her firm, Risa Heller worked for Jane Harman, then a Democratic congresswoman from California, and later served as Democratic senator Chuck Schumer’s New York City press secretary. Nvidia’s head of public policy communications, John Rizzo, also previously worked for Schumer, serving as his deputy communications director.

"The lesson learned is you can have both": Skyrim lead designer reckons there's merit in Baldur's Gate 3-style tough choices and Bethesda's do-anything approach

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

How many doors should be open at any point in an adventure?

Whether RPGs are better off prioritising player freedom or endeavouring to have several doors slam shut every time you put another foot forwards in order to encourage multiple runs with different characters is, to put it bluntly, something I reckon will still be a matter of debate in the year 4000. We'll all have evolved in non-corporeal beings floating in a galaxy that's been racked by such change that even the six million copies of The Elder Scrolls 5 we've littered the Earth with will have been swept away into the great recycling bin on the gods' desktop, and there'll still be sniping going on about whether it's ok that Skyrim lets you be a master thiefssassin who leads the companions, knows every spell under the sun, and slapped up a world-eating dragon for fun.

For what it's worth, the game's lead designer reckons both games that do and don't let you do that have their merits when executed well.

Speaking to The Examined Game, ex-Bethesda dev Bruce Nesmith talked about being "pleasantly surprised" when playing Baldur's Gate 3 just how much he enjoyed making "consequential decisions that closed off gameplay". In Nesmith's view, Larian "redefined things in a certain way" with their take on the D&D universe and the fantasy rules it plays by. However, he argued that there's still merit to taking an approach more akin to Bethesda's post-Morrowind Elder Scrolls entries and striving to make it possible for the player to see the majority of what the game has to offer in a single playthrough.

"They were both fun," he said. "They were just fun in different ways and that to me was part of the surprise. I would have expected to fall on one side or the other, as if they were things that you couldn't have both [of]. The lesson learned is you can have both. I can enjoy this game over here that does it this way and enjoy this game over here that does it that way."

Nesmith made clear that he still thinks the idea of, with a few exceptions like Skyrim's civil war, not gating the player off from the likes of doing multiple faction questlines is a "very good philosophy" that he supports.

As much as it's always a bit of a cop out to declare that everything's equally good, actually, I do think that there's merit in both approaches. I love the dedication the likes of New Vegas, Disco Elysium, and The Witcher 2 have to making you pick a path and see where it takes you at the expense of not seeing other stuff. Hell, the latter game's infamous for being totally different depending on who you choose to waltz off with at the end of the opening act.

That said, Skyrim's open-endedness was undoubtedly one of the factors which made it so easy for me to get majorly into when I was just cutting my teeth in RPGs as a teenager. There's value in such games existing, even if for plenty it might be as a gateway drug to games with crunchier and more challenging narratives that do demand you plug in your brain and carefully consider every choice you make. Whether there's still as much value in being the gateway drug nowadays compared to in 2011 is the question The Elder Scrolls 6'll likely answer whenever it rocks up. Coming as it is after the likes of BG3 and The Witcher 3 have had near Skyrim-level gangbusters success while leaning a lot more heavily into the hard stuff typically pegged as having less potential for mass appeal.

For what it's worth, while you can very much argue about the execution, Fallout 4's main quest was a lot more choice-heavy than Fallout 3's. So, Bethesda may well opt to pivot or make compromises in their player freedom-first ways if they feel some crunchier choice is in order.

Russell Brand Sentenced to More Than 10 Years in Prison After Rape Conviction

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Russell Brand was sentenced to 10 years and six months in prison Monday following his conviction on rape and sexual assault charges.

A jury in London found Brand guilty on four of the seven charges against him, including one count of rape and three counts of sexual assault. He was found not guilty on two additional counts of rape, as well as one count of attempted indecent assault. The allegations against him were tied to claims made by six women between 1999 and 2009.

The bulk of Brand’s sentence was tied to the rape charge, for which the judge sentenced him to nine years in prison. Meanwhile, each of the three assault charges carried their own penalty: nine months, 18 months, and eight months. The assault sentences will be served concurrently, but consecutively with the sentence for the rape charge.

Brand will eventually be eligible for parole, but only after serving two-thirds of his sentence. That means he will spend at least seven years behind bars, and, if granted parole, would have to serve the remainder of his sentence under supervision and abiding by specific rules.

Prior to handing down Brand’s prison term, Judge Joel Bennathan explained how he would approach sentencing and recounted much of the testimony given by the women whose claims led to Brand’s conviction. In many instances, Bennathan was unsparing. He called Brand a sexual predator on several occasions, criticized the comedian’s “staggering arrogance,” and reprimanded his “belief that fame meant you could act how you like, when you liked, unhindered by the rules by which decent people live” (via The Guardian).

Still, the judge acknowledged several mitigating factors he considered when deciding Brand’s sentence. These included the work Brand has done helping those struggling with addiction, several character witnesses, and the fact that Brand has not been accused of any other crimes in the 17 years.

Right before handing down Brand’s sentence, Bennathan said he wanted to “pay tribute to the determination and bravery of all the woman whose evidence I have been recounting.” He added, “I do not increase your punishment because of it but all those women had to face being confronted and being told they lied, or twisted the truth, and or they’d agreed to your sexual demands. That was all done because of you, and your instructions.” Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

Speaking with reporters outside the court, Brand’s father, Ron Brand, suggested his son was the victim, and referred to his accusers as “The girls who chased him and harassed him.” He dismissed the conviction and justice system as well, saying “it’s possible” that the jury in the case was corrupt (via Sky News).

The rape count, the most severe charge Brand was found guilty of, had a maximum sentence of life imprisonment under British sentencing guidelines, but the more common term was four to 19 years behind bars.

Following Brand’s conviction on Thursday, the actor was remanded into custody; he had been out on bail during his trial. “A custodial sentence of some length will result,” the judge told Brand.

The rape conviction stemmed from a 2009 incident where a woman said in a police interview that she went to Brand’s home expecting to have consensual vaginal sex, but during intercourse Brand removed his condom and had anal sex with her. “I said, ‘Can you stop?’” she told police of the incident. “I said it repeatedly.” The woman told some of her friends about the incident soon after, who later testified during Brand’s trial. Brand testified that the anal sex was consensual and she never told him to stop.

After Brand’s rape trial, London’s Met Police announced that two more women had come forward with allegations against the actor, which could result in more charges against Brand and another trial. Their investigation is ongoing.

Google reportedly adds two TPU variants for secure and long-context AI

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Google’s headquarters in Mountain View, California.

Google is introducing two new versions of its in-house AI chips, known as tensor processing units (TPUs), Bloomberg News reported on Monday.

The chips are called the TPU 8tx and the TPU 8i Max. Amin Vahdat, Google’s chief technologist for AI infrastructure, told Bloomberg they are reworked versions of existing chips, built for workloads that customers had asked for.

The 8tx is designed for secure and confidential work used by government agencies and companies in regulated industries. It runs alongside chips from AMD as part of a partnership between the two companies, according to Bloomberg’s report.

The 8i Max, by contrast, is built for AI applications that need long context windows. That means they can take in very large amounts of text or data in a single request.

Both are based on Google’s eighth-generation chips, the TPU 8t and TPU 8i, which the company unveiled at its Cloud Next event in April. The 8t is built to train AI models, while the 8i runs them once they are trained. Google said both would be generally available later this year.

Google used to release one new AI processor every two years, according to Bloomberg. This year it launched two at once, and the new variants take the eighth-generation line to four chips. Vahdat said each one is tuned for different needs.

Google first built TPUs for its own use. They have since become one of the main alternatives to Nvidia’s graphics processors, which dominate AI computing.

Google rents out TPUs through its cloud business rather than selling them as standalone chips. Anthropic agreed last year to use up to 1 million of them.

The company is also widening its chip supply chain. It works with four design partners, including Broadcom and MediaTek, to build its TPUs.

According to Bloomberg, the new variants are meant to serve an industry that is moving towards AI agents. These are AI tools that carry out tasks on a user’s behalf. At the same time, companies face growing security risks as they put such tools to work.

Is There Really a Link Between Genius and Madness? A Study of 14,658 Twins Just Uncovered a Surprise.

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Artists showed a clear link to poorer mental health. Scientists didn’t—and the reason may be unsettling.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

Aristotle once said that “no great mind has ever existed without a touch of madness.” While that millennia-old hypothesis doesn’t hold up to modern scientific scrutiny, researchers are interested in exploring the relationship between artistic and scientific creativity and mental health. While there are many high-profile artists who’ve very publicly struggled with mental health (Vincent van Gogh cut his own ear off, after all), those cases are purely anecdotal. Does scientific evidence suggest there’s really a correlation?

Getting closer to an answer to that age-old question was the primary goal of a new study published in the journal Molecular Psychiatry that was led by researchers at the Max Planck Institute for Empirical Aesthetics and Karolinska Institutet in Sweden.

“There is a longstanding idea that genius and ‘madness’ go hand in hand, particularly in creative fields, with many famous artists believed to have struggled with mental health problems,” Karolinska Institutet’s Miriam A. Mosing, a co-senior author of the study, told Medical Xpress. "However, the empirical evidence for this association remains mixed.”

To even attempt to understand this possible mental correlation, the team needed an immensely large dataset. Luckily, they could pore over two surveys conducted in 2012–2013 and 2022–2023 that collected mental health data from 14,658 individual twins in Sweden, fewer than half of whom participated in both surveys. The first survey assessed artistic and scientific achievement and noted self-reported psychiatric symptoms and openness to experience, while the second one tracked divergent thinking, or the ability to solve complex problems.

“This idea is fueled by many anecdotal examples of famous creators with eccentric personalities and psychiatric problems, from Vincent van Gogh and Ernest Hemingway to Virginia Woolf and Camille Claudel,” the authors wrote in the study. “Research including some systematic reviews and meta-analyses, has suggested that creativity and engagement in creative activities are associated with an increased risk of mental health problems.”

The study also aimed to assess the role genetics and environment play on this correlation, and that’s where the twins aspect of the dataset comes in handy. With identical twins, for example, researchers could effectively remove genetic and environmental factors and assess whether the associations remained.

They found that artistic creativity did have a positive correlation with poorer mental health, much as previous studies indicated. However, scientific creativity didn’t hold a similar correlation. One theory, proposed by the authors, suggests that scientific environments are “more unforgiving” of mental health problems than artistic ones are, leading to indirect selection processes. Interestingly, the team also found a positive correlation between depressive symptoms and openness, suggesting that an introspective personality might lead one to question social norms. They also noted that genes appeared to play a significant role.

“Though our study suggested the importance of genes, it doesn’t mean environment is not important,” Max Planck Institute’s Penghao Xia, lead author of the study, told Medical XPress. “Therefore, we still need to investigate other explanations of the association."

Eli Lilly’s Foundayo obesity pill eases sleep apnea in late-stage trial, paving way for approval

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Eli Lilly on Monday said its obesity pill Foundayo significantly reduced the severity of obstructive sleep apnea, the most common sleep-related breathing disorder, in patients with obesity, in a late-stage trial.

The drugmaker plans to file by the end of the year for U.S. approval of the daily pill for moderate-to-severe obstructive sleep apnea, or OSA, in adults with obesity or who are overweight. The results make the pill — which rolled out in the U.S. in April — one of several GLP-1 treatments to show promise in treating the condition.

OSA refers to interrupted breathing during sleep due to narrowed or blocked airways, which can cause drops in oxygen and increases in blood pressure that are associated with serious complications, such as hypertension, heart failure and stroke. The condition affects nearly 1 billion adults globally, including 80 million people ages 20 and over in the U.S., according to the National Council on Aging. Most have never been diagnosed, Lilly said in a release.

Lilly's blockbuster weight loss injection Zepbound became the first medication to win U.S. approval for OSA in December 2024. Patients have limited treatment options outside of that drug and many use cumbersome machines that provide positive airway pressure, or PAP, to allow for normal breathing.

"Foundayo is already making a difference for people living with obesity, and with plans to submit these results to the FDA by the end of the year, we could have the opportunity to help people living with OSA," Dr. Thomas Seck, senior vice president of product development of Lilly Cardiometabolic Health, said in a release.

The trial tested Foundayo in two independent studies, the first of which included 363 patients who were unable or unwilling to use PAP therapy. The second study included 349 people who were on PAP therapy for at least three months at the time of screening and continued that treatment during the trial.

Patients in both studies who took Foundayo started at a low dose and increased it gradually at four-week intervals to different maximum dosages.

Researchers specifically examined how much the pill reduced the so-called apnea-hypopnea index, or AHI, which records the number of times per hour a person's breathing shows a restricted or completely blocked airway. The index is used to evaluate the severity of obstructive sleep apnea and the effectiveness of treatments for the condition.

People who took Foundayo and were not using PAP therapy had an average of 21.1 fewer AHI events per hour at 52 weeks, compared with an average reduction of 4.8 per hour for the placebo, according to the results.

Foundayo also met the trial's second goal in that group, leading to an overall AHI reduction of 43.8% compared with a decrease of 6.3% for the placebo. Lilly said 31.5% of the participants in the Foundayo group met the criteria for remission or improvement to mild OSA.

Meanwhile, patients who were on both Foundayo and PAP therapy had an average of 18.3 fewer AHI events per hour at 52 weeks, compared with an average reduction of 5.5 per hour for the placebo.

Foundayo led to an overall AHI reduction of around 27.5% in that group, compared with a decrease of 3% for the placebo. The company said 19.6% of the participants on Foundayo and PAP therapy also met the criteria for remission or mild OSA.

Foundayo also reduced what's known as the sleep apnea-specific hypoxic burden — a measure of how deeply and for how long oxygen levels fall during sleep. That metric fell by 55.9% in people not using PAP therapy and 45.8% in those using it.

Lilly said the pill also helped patients lose roughly 11% of their weight across groups at 52 weeks and helped to lower a marker of inflammation.

The company said the safety data and how well patients tolerated Foundayo were generally consistent with previous trials on the pill.

Around 12% of people on Foundayo who were not using PAP therapy discontinued due to side effects, compared to 3.8% with the placebo. Meanwhile, 6.8% of those on both Lilly's pill and PAP discontinued the drug due to side effects, compared with 0.6% in the placebo group.

The most common side effects were gastrointestinal, including nausea, diarrhea, constipation and vomiting, and were generally mild to moderate in severity. That's consistent with other GLP-1 medications.

SpaceX will launch epic double-catch Starship Flight 15 test mission on Nov. 13

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The company apparently isn't worried about flying on Friday the 13th.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

SpaceX will make spaceflight history this week, if all goes according to plan.

The company announced over the weekend that it's targeting Friday (Nov. 13) for the 15th test flight of its Starship megarocket, which will attempt something that's never been done before.

"The upcoming flight is planned to be the first to return both Starship and Super Heavy to the launch site for catch," SpaceX wrote in a description of Flight 15. "If successful, this will mark the first full recovery of an orbital launch system and a breakthrough moment in making Starship a reliable, rapidly reusable rocket."

Super Heavy is Starship's giant booster, and Starship (or Ship for short) is its upper-stage spacecraft. As SpaceX's description noted, both elements are designed to be fully and rapidly reusable.

"Catch" refers to SpaceX's recovery mechanism for both stages: Each will return to the launch mount at SpaceX's Starbase site in South Texas and be plucked out of the sky by the tower's "chopstick" arms.

SpaceX has caught Super Heavy on three Starship flights to date, but it has never tried the maneuver with Ship. It's a taller order; Ship flies higher and farther than Super Heavy, so its return to Earth is more complex and energetic.

SpaceX founder and CEO Elon Musk already told us that Flight 15 would likely feature a double-catch attempt. But the newly released mission description gives us a launch date, as well as details about the flight.

Starship Die Cast Rocket Model Was $47.99 Now $39.99 on Amazon.

Even if you can't see SpaceX's Starship in person, you can score a model of your own. Standing at 13.77 inches (35 cm), this is a 1:375 ratio of SpaceX's Starship as a desktop model. The materials here are alloy steel and it weighs just 225g.

Flight 15 is scheduled to launch from Starbase on Friday during a 75-minute window that opens at 5:45 p.m. EST (2245 GMT; 4:45 p.m. local Texas time).

Super Heavy will come back to Starbase for its tower catch about 6.5 minutes after liftoff, if all goes to plan. But Ship will stay up quite a bit longer.

The upper stage will go orbital, something Starship did for the first time on Flight 14 on Sept. 28. And, as on Flight 14, Ship will deploy 26 of SpaceX's next-gen Starlink V3 satellites, the vanguard of a new megaconstellation that will eventually consist of about 100,000 spacecraft, according to Musk.

But Ship should stay aloft for much longer on Flight 15 than it did on Flight 14, which ended with a splashdown in the northern Pacific Ocean about three hours after liftoff.

"Starship’s initial return to launch site mission is expected to orbit the Earth at an altitude of ~275 km [171 miles] and complete approximately 14 orbits around the planet over the course of a more than 22-hour flight," SpaceX wrote in its description of Flight 15.

"Similar to Starship’s first orbital mission, the flight profile has been designed with the opportunity to insert into a passively safe suborbital trajectory or safely deorbit at multiple pre-determined areas over the ocean after raising to an orbital altitude," the company added.

So there's no guarantee that Ship will indeed come back to Starbase for a catch on Flight 15; SpaceX will call off the try if anything is amiss. Whatever happens, however, you should definitely tune in. It's not every day, after all, that you can watch a rocket the size of a 40-story building take flight.

Who Gives A Crap? Hackers, apparently, after toilet paper maker's customer data exposed

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

UK shoppers caught up in third-party security mess, and nobody knows whether the data was flushed away

Toilet paper purveyor Who Gives A Crap has found itself in a bit of a mess after an intruder accessed customer information through a third-party software provider, leaving the company scrambling to clean up a breach affecting shoppers in the UK, US, and Australia.

In an email sent to affected customers over the weekend, seen by The Register, the loo roll seller warns that personal information was accessed without authorization after a security incident at an unnamed US-based marketing software provider.

The email explains that the intruder gained access to the supplier's systems on October 30. The provider spotted the intrusion the following day and says it secured its systems by November 1, although Who Gives A Crap wasn't told until November 6. It's unclear why the supplier sat on the bad news for another five days before passing it on.

The exposed information includes customers' names, email addresses, phone numbers, mailing addresses, and total spending with the company. In other words, someone may now know not only where you live, but just how much of your hard-earned cash you've flushed down the crapper.

Payment information, order details, and passwords were not involved, and customers' accounts were not directly accessed. That's something of a relief, although whether the intruder actually made off with the data remains a pressing question.

"We don't yet know whether the information was copied or downloaded," the company told customers, adding that it had found no evidence of misuse.

In a separate public statement, Who Gives A Crap co-founder and CEO Simon Griffiths confirmed that the incident affected customers across the company's Australian, UK, and US stores. Canadian customers, meanwhile, appear to have escaped this particular toilet emergency, with the supplier reporting that no personal information belonging to shoppers in the country was accessed.

Who Gives A Crap says it has since disconnected the provider from its systems, changed access credentials, and begun warning affected customers to watch out for suspicious emails, texts, calls, and other unsolicited communications.

"The main concern is scam or phishing messages that use your real details to look convincing," the company warned in its customer email.

While Who Gives A Crap has been busy cleaning up, the supplier at the center of this particular stink remains a mystery. The company hasn't identified the software provider or disclosed how many customers were affected, including those in the UK, and didn’t respond to our questions.

Griffiths said the company is "engaging with the relevant regulatory authorities, as required." In the UK, the ICO told The Register: "We can confirm Who Gives A Crap Limited has reported an incident and we are assessing the information provided."

Who Gives A Crap has plenty of toilet paper to hand, although cleaning up this particular situation might require more than a few rolls. ®

Whose tradition, which history? Agency adjudication and Sun Valley Orchards

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

Over the past few decades, several features of the administrative state have been subject to heated constitutional challenge. One of them is agency adjudication: the practice of resolving disputes “in-house” within administrative agencies instead of addressing these disputes directly in a court. Of late, the Supreme Court has applied a “history-and-tradition” methodology to assess the constitutionality of agency adjudication. But that methodology confronts the challenge of how to select from among multiple competing traditions the one that should carry the day. On Nov. 10, the court will hear a case, Department of Labor v. Sun Valley Orchards, that underscores the difficulties of carrying out its history-and-tradition methodology – difficulties that apply across many areas of law.

By way of background, the basic constitutional issue regarding administrative adjudication is as follows. Article III of the Constitution vests the “judicial power of the United States” in the Supreme Court and lower federal courts. From the founding and onward, however, disputes that fall within the jurisdiction of Article III courts have been heard in tribunals belonging to the other federal branches, such as territorial courts, military tribunals, bankruptcy courts, magistrate judges, and most pertinent for current purposes, administrative tribunals – for instance, by administrative law judges.

Agency adjudication of, say, Social Security and veterans’ benefits claims is an integral part of the administrative state. It provides a streamlined way of handling these claims and avoids clogging up the Article III courts. Although there is usually a right to appeal administrative determinations at some point to Article III courts, these courts apply a deferential standard of review.

The question arises whether administrative adjudication is compatible with the judicial scheme laid out in Article III of the Constitution. This is a practically significant issue; agency dispute resolution has substantial effects on individuals and companies, who can face considerable fines or penalties. To what extent, then, is agency adjudication outside Article III courts justified? And what kinds of limitations should be placed on it?

In the past few decades, the Supreme Court has turned to “history and tradition” to answer these questions. History and tradition as methods of constitutional interpretation are likely most familiar from cases on hot-button social issues, such as Dobbs (holding that there was no “history and tradition” of a right to have an abortion) and Bruen (holding that there was no “history and tradition” of requiring certain types of licenses to carry firearms outside the home). But the court has also drawn on history and tradition to shape the scope of federal judicial power, in contexts ranging from Article III standing to equitable remedies. One such setting is the division of labor between Article III courts and agency adjudicative bodies.

History and tradition play a dual role in the law of adjudication outside Article III courts. This pattern was on display in the Supreme Court’s 2024 case Securities and Exchange Commission v. Jarkesy, which addressed the constitutionality of agency adjudication of certain claims involving securities fraud. First, tradition provides a justification for non-Article III (including agency) adjudication of a limited class of claims deemed to involve “public rights,” that is, matters which historically “could have been determined exclusively by [the executive and legislative] branches.” In Jarkesy, the court listed “the collection of revenue,” “immigration,” “tariffs,” “relations with Indian tribes,” “the administration of public lands,” and “the granting of public benefits such as payments to veterans, pensions, and patent rights” as members of this class of claims. Since these matters, the court explained, historically could have been adjudicated outside Article III courts, they may be heard by non-Article III tribunals today.

Second, tradition provides a reason that claims involving “private” rather than “public” rights must be brought before Article III courts instead of administrative tribunals. A hallmark of a private-rights suit that belongs before an Article III court, the Jarkesy court stated, is being “made of the stuff of the traditional actions at common law tried by the courts at Westminster in 1789.”

In Jarkesy, the court decided that the securities fraud claim at issue resembled common-law fraud, a traditional common-law claim and so a private right requiring an Article III tribunal. (The court also emphasized that the remedy the SEC was pursuing – civil penalties – was historically enforceable in courts of law; accordingly, the SEC was bringing a suit “at common law” to which the Seventh Amendment jury trial right attached. Jarkesy thus revolved substantially around the jury trial right, which is not a question directly presented in Sun Valley.)

Tradition, then, helps to demarcate the boundary between claims that must be adjudicated by Article III courts (private rights) and claims that may be resolved before other courts, including administrative agencies (public rights). But how should the court decide whether a present-day claim fits into a tradition of private rights or public rights? In particular, what should the court do when there are multiple traditions into which a present-day claim might fit?

The court’s upcoming case Sun Valley provides an example of the problem of “too many traditions.” Sun Valley involves a nonimmigrant visa program, administered jointly by the Department of Labor and the Department of Homeland Security, that permits American employers to hire foreign laborers to perform temporary or seasonal agricultural work. As a condition of participating in the program, employers agree to provide certain workplace arrangements to both foreign and domestic workers. DOL found that Sun Valley, a New Jersey farm, had failed to provide these arrangements, and the agency assessed hundreds of thousands of dollars in civil penalties and back wages against Sun Valley. Sun Valley was required to contest these penalties before an administrative law judge, with deferential review by an Article III court. Sun Valley challenged the administrative-adjudication requirement as a violation of Article III, and the U.S. Court of Appeals for the 3rd Circuit agreed with Sun Valley.

In Supreme Court briefing, the government and Sun Valley both appeal to the history-and-tradition methodology of Jarkesy, but they sharply disagree about which tradition is relevant. The government emphasizes traditions related to immigration. According to the government, its enforcement action fits within two historical categories of public rights adjudication that did not require Article III courts: rights “belonging to the sovereign, such as the right to determine whether to allow aliens into the country,” and “privileges conferred by the government, such as public benefits granted under government programs.” In the government’s view, Sun Valley violated the conditions of bringing foreign workers to the United States through an immigration program that offered the farm a benefit. The pertinent tradition is hence the history of adjudicating immigration- and benefits- related claims outside Article III courts.

Sun Valley, by contrast, casts its claim as akin to common-law breach of contract – a traditional private right requiring an Article III tribunal. DOL’s allegation, Sun Valley observes, was that Sun Valley had violated its agreement to provide certain working conditions to foreign and domestic workers. That allegation, Sun Valley contends, is simply a charge that Sun Valley breached a contract. Sun Valley further argues that DOL sought common-law remedies and in particular civil penalties – more evidence that the claim at issue involves a private right. To Sun Valley, the case is located outside the historical tradition of immigration adjudication because it is based on conditions of employment rather than immigration status. Consequently, it was unconstitutional for DOL to adjudicate Sun Valley’s dispute in-house.

Which party is right about the tradition into which the dispute over Sun Valley’s working conditions falls? Plausibly, the answer is “both.” Yes, the government is alleging that Sun Valley violated an agreement; but the agreement in question was concluded as part of an immigration program allowing Sun Valley to hire foreign workers.

So what are courts to do when, as here, they are faced with “too many traditions” – that is, traditions with different implications for the constitutional question at hand?

One possible response is to conclude that history-and-tradition analysis is hopelessly indeterminate and to reject this methodology. While many methods of constitutional interpretation fail to yield a single right answer, “history and tradition” is often justified precisely by its promise to produce constraint and fixity. Breaking this promise might be viewed as grounds for abandoning the history-and-tradition approach.

Complete repudiation of history and tradition, however, would be unwarranted. Historical practice has played an important role in both upholding and limiting non-Article III adjudication; it is a tool in judges’ toolbox and should not be thrown away.

Another option is to supplement history-and-tradition methodology, when it does not produce a clear result, with other modes of constitutional analysis. Earlier cases on non-Article III adjudication, for instance, appealed openly to practical considerations of government efficiency.

A third option would be to draw on other “modalities” of constitutional interpretation to inform the choice of which strand of tradition to emphasize. One such modality is precedent. Jarkesy did not directly confront a program like the DOL’s in Sun Valley, so it is not controlling precedent. But Jarkesy did seem to treat Congress’ broad power over immigration as a reason to uphold agency adjudication in immigration-related cases. Perhaps this suggests that DOL’s enforcement action fits better into a traditional public-rights than private-rights category.

The court may also decide to avoid the problem of too many traditions by taking a statutory “out.” When it took the case, the court added a question regarding DOL’s statutory authority to impose civil penalties and back wages on Sun Valley. The court may decide that DOL lacks such statutory authority, potentially by appealing to constitutional avoidance (in other words, construing the statute to grant DOL such authority would raise serious questions about the constitutionality of agency adjudication, which should be avoided unless necessary to decide). But the statutory question is not straightforward, and resolving it may well have ramifications for a wide array of agency-imposed penalties. At any rate, one or more of the justices can be expected to opine on Article III in Sun Valley, and the Article III issue is bound to recur.

Overall, the problem of “too many traditions” is by no means unique to the context of non-Article III adjudication. The Supreme Court faces challenging questions about how to apply history-and-tradition methodology when a present-day practice could plausibly fit into multiple traditions. The court’s response to these questions may have an important impact not only on the future of agency adjudication, but also on the trajectory of historically driven interpretation across a variety of pressing constitutional debates.

Rachel Bayefsky is Professor of Law at the University of Virginia School of Law, where she writes about civil procedure, constitutional law, and legal theory. She clerked for Justice Ruth Bader Ginsburg in the 2018-2019 Term.

Recommended Citation: Rachel Bayefsky, Whose tradition, which history? Agency adjudication and Sun Valley Orchards, SCOTUSblog (Nov. 9, 2026, 9:30 AM), https://www.scotusblog.com/2026/11/whose-tradition-which-history-agency-adjudication-and-sun-valley-orchards/

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/alignment-healthcare-sues-cms-over-its-2027-medicare-advantage-star-ratings" hreflang="en">Elevance Health sues over 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings</a>

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →
Elevance Health sues over 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Elevance Health is the latest insurer to sue the feds over its performance in the latest round of star ratings for the Medicare Advantage program.

The company sued the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services in District of Columbia federal court on Friday, arguing that the 2027 star ratings calculations violated the Administrative Procedure Act in using 11 measures that it says were adopted unlawfully.

In the lawsuit, the company said that the harm from including these measures is "concrete and substantial." If they were excluded, it would increase the scores for 12 of its Medicare Advantage contracts and generate $516 million in additional quality bonus payments.

"Their inclusion therefore does more than violate statutory requirements in the abstract: it depresses Elevance Health’s ratings and reduces the funding available for the following payment year," per the lawsuit. "That funding could support broader supplemental benefits, lower premiums and cost-sharing, and improved care coordination."

"CMS’s unlawful methodology puts those resources out of reach," the company said.

In addition to the financial impacts, Elevance argues that the star ratings scores can also lead to reputational damage that makes it harder for them to compete in the MA market. It also provides prospective enrollees with inaccurate information, the company said.

"Beneficiaries cannot make informed coverage choices when the government’s own comparison tool conveys a distorted assessment of the plans available to them," Elevance said.

"An artificially depressed rating does not merely place Elevance Health at a competitive disadvantage; it undermines the very comparison the program is supposed to enable," the company continued. "A rating lowered by unlawful measures presents beneficiaries with a misleading signal of relative quality, rather than a lawful basis for distinguishing among competing plans."

CVS Heath's Aetna is joining the ranks of insurers suing over its results in the 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings.

The insured filed suit Monday in Georgia's Southern District Court, and argues that 28 measures used by the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services to calculate its scores were "unlawfully adopted" and should have been excluded.

The court is the same venue that ruled in favor of Clover Health over the summer, and set aside 20 measures that were used to determine its 2026 scores. That decision, which the feds have appealed, kicked off a broad recalculation of 2026 scores that looked different for insurers outside of Clover, triggering a wave of litigation.

In its lawsuit, Aetna cites the Clover decision in finding that CMS extended beyond its statutory authority in deploying those 20 measures.

The company said that calculating its scores using the 28 measures in question will likely cost it $2 billion in 2028 quality bonus payments and rebate retention. Those are funds that the insurer could "apply toward expanding member benefits, reducing premiums and investing in quality improvement."

Aetna adds that in addition to the monetary loss, having lower scores negatively impacts the company's reputation and its ability to attract potential members.

"The harm to CVS Health is not exclusively financial; lower ratings carry meaningful reputational harm, which can in turn impact membership," per the lawsuit. "By incorporating unlawful measures into the 2027 Star Ratings calculation, CMS cast CVS Health’s ratings in a poorer light in the eyes of Medicare Advantage consumers relative to peer MAOs, undermining CVS Health’s ability to compete for Medicare beneficiaries through enhanced benefits and the like."

Aetna did not respond to a request for comment at the time of publication.

Based on an analysis from Stephens, 69% of Aetna's MA members are in plans with four or more stars for 2027, down from 81%. That threshold is the key to securing quality bonus payments.

Aetna's complaint follows others focused on the 2027 scores from Centene and Alignment Healthcare (see those stories below).

Centene has become the latest insurer to sue the federal government over its 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings.

In a lawsuit filed Thursday in Georgia's Southern District Court, the company argues that the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services violated the Administrative Procedure Act in determining its latest scores. Centene says that CMS ran afoul of "core statutory safeguards" in deploying 17 measures.

The use of those measures ultimately caused "significant injury" to the company, according to the complaint. For 2027, 9% of Centene's members are enrolled in plans with at least four stars, which declined from 18% in the prior year.

"In implementing each of the individual Star measures that Centene challenges, CMS violated applicable law and thereby caused Centene to lose out on hundreds of millions of dollars in revenue that Centene could use to strengthen benefits, reduce premiums, and improve health outcomes," the company said in the lawsuit.

CMS argues that the court has already sided with a similar argument in its decision in favor of Clover Health earlier this year. In that case, which led to a broad recalculation in scores, the courts set aside 20 measures that were used in determining Clover's star ratings for 2026.

If the 17 measures in question had been excluded in calculating Centene's scores, the company estimates it would receive $448 million more in quality bonus payments for the 2028 plan year, funds it would deploy to expand benefits, lower premiums and enhance programs like care coordination.

In addition to the financial impact, a drop in star ratings also carries a reputational hit, Centene said.

"Beyond the financial impact to the company and its members, depressed ratings also inflict significant reputational damage and diminished membership," the company said in the complaint

Centene had not returned a request for comment at the time of publication.

Alignment Healthcare sued federal regulators, alleging that its 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings were determined "in flagrant violation" of statute.

In the lawsuit filed Friday in District of Columbia federal court, the insurer says that the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services "has completely departed from Congress’s vision of leveraging predictable, valid measures developed with input from providers, plans, and the public" in establishing the methodology for the star ratings.

The company argues that rather than establish "consistent, stable specifications" for metrics through the traditional rule-making process, the agency instead detailed key measures through a series of "technical notes." This violates the Administrative Procedure Act, per the lawsuit.

The program's star ratings are the critical determinant in quality bonus payments to insurers as well as a tool that aims to make it easier for enrollees and brokers to identify benefits with the highest quality.

"Consistent application of the statutory requirements governing Star Ratings is therefore essential not only to plan payments and competition but also to ensuring that beneficiaries can rely on the Star Ratings as meaningful and comparable measures of plan quality," Alignment said in the lawsuit.

The company adds that multiple recent court decisions have set aside CMS' star ratings methodology, notably a ruling over the summer in favor of Clover Health that tossed 20 metrics used in the 2026 calculations. A Georgia federal judge determined that 10 measures utilized exceeded CMS' authority, while 10 other measures were promulgated inappropriately.

CMS recalculated the scores across the board following the ruling, but only set aside the 10 measures that the courts determined were outside the bounds of its regulatory authority. The agency maintained that approach in the 2027 calculations, Alignment argues.

"Congress created the Star Ratings program to allow Medicare Advantage beneficiaries to make informed decisions about plan enrollment based on clinically validated measures of plan quality that had been subjected to notice and comment," the company said. "CMS’s contrary approach fails to carry out the vision that Congress set forth by statute."

The Trump administration has appealed the Clover decision, so that legal case remains ongoing.

In addition, Alignment argues it suffered reputational and financial damage as a result of its 2027 scores, which plummeted compared to the prior year. Just 25% of its members are in plans with four or more stars for 2027, down from 100% in 2026.

Shares in the company tumbled following the release of the ratings, though stock prices have rebounded since.

Alignment sued the feds following the Clover decision to secure a recalculation for its scores that aligns with the court's decision, and in a statement to Fierce Healthcare, said that the lawsuit aligns with that perspective.

"Our lawsuit seeks to ensure that the Star Ratings program operates as Congress intended and that Alignment is properly compensated for the care we provide," Alignment said in the statement. "We respect the judicial process and will present our case in court. Because this matter involves pending litigation, we will not comment on the specific legal arguments beyond our filings."

"Our focus remains on delivering exceptional care, improving health outcomes and advocating for a stable, lawful regulatory framework that supports seniors and strengthens the Medicare Advantage program," the company said.

STAT+: Eli Lilly’s GLP-1 pill Foundayo helped alleviate sleep apnea in studies

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Chen, a national biotech reporter, is the co-author of The Readout, a newsletter about the business, science, and politics of biotech.

Elaine’s stories explore the boom in new obesity treatments, new drug payment models, the ways companies are affected by FDA changes, and the emerging psychedelics drug sector. She co-writes The Readout newsletter, and co-hosts STAT’s weekly biotech podcast, The Readout Loud. You can reach Elaine on Signal at elaineywchen.70.

Eli Lilly’s GLP-1 pill Foundayo helped alleviate sleep apnea in two late-stage studies, but it doesn’t appear as effective as Lilly’s injectable drug Zepbound.

In a one-year trial of patients who weren’t receiving PAP therapy, a form of ventilation, those on Foundayo saw a reduction of 17.8 “events” per hour on the apnea-hypopnea index (AHI), a measure of the number of times breathing stops and becomes restricted while sleeping. That compares with a reduction of 4.5 events among those on placebo.

Additionally, 28% of treated patients achieved remission or mild disease, compared with 13% in the placebo group, according to data presented Monday at the annual meeting of the American Heart Association and published in JAMA.

'The Best RPG I've Played This Year' - Queen's Domain Is A Low-Key GOTY Contender On Xbox

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Back at the end of last month, a little-known RPG launched on Xbox in the form of Queen's Domain, which we highlighted in our October 30th new releases roundup alongside 14 other titles that arrived that day.

Ever since then, we've been hearing various bits of praise for the game across multiple platforms, and it's gotten to the point where it's definitely a Game of the Year contender - albeit a very low-key one.

Officially, what you're getting here is a "retro-fantasy action RPG with brutal combat and traversal-focused exploration", and Queen's Domain is being compared to loads of legendary franchises including King's Field, Zelda and Dark Souls.

Here's what just a few people are saying about it on Steam, for example:

cautious asparagus on Steam: "It's the kind of game that keeps surprising you with the sheer scope and creativity. You know the first time in Elden Ring where you find the elevator that brings you down to Siofra River, and you're just in awe of how much more there is to the game? There are multiple times in Queen's Domain where you feel that same way. This is the best RPG I have played this year, and it might even be my game of the year."

Huell Babineaux: "Everyone says this is one of the best kings field successors out there and it probably is, but ive never played those games, so to me this game feels more like a dark and gritty first person legend of zelda, and it pulls this off exceptionally well. Combat is brutal and unforgiving, but also learnable. Exploration can be daunting and overwhelming, but always rewarding.

Powersteve on Steam: My game of the year. Not just the best King's Field imitator but a genuine improvement on the formula. Better combat, better variety of combat options and a massive world with some of the best level and secret design I have ever seen. Also remains engaging and balanced for the whole duration and doesn't turn into Doom with lightning bolts half way through like Lunacid. So good that I can't wait to get all the endings just so I can use those secrets to do a fresh run with sequence breaks which is something that I almost never do.

So, why haven't we mentioned this before? Well, the Xbox version of Queen's Domain was actually getting a bit of flack at launch, as people were pointing out a stuttering issue that seemed to be plaguing the Series X and S versions.

Looking at the reviews on the Xbox Store now though, the issue seems to have been addressed in a recent patch, and Queen's Domain is currently rocking a 4.7/5 rating on the US version of the Xbox Store as things stand.

In other words, this might well be worth picking up for your Xbox console! It's £16.74 / $19.99 if you fancy taking a punt in the near future, and we'll leave you with more details and screenshots for it down below.

Queen’s Domain is a retro-fantasy action RPG. Washed up on the shores of the Isle of Neasied, you set out to find your father and uncover the dark heart of this mysterious isle. Wield the Legendary Holy Sword Endu to take flight from platform to platform, and discover an interconnected, labyrinthine world where secrets and dangers lurk around every corner.

Fight action-oriented battles with a classic stamina meter, powerful charge attacks, weapon specific special attacks, and secondary throwing weapons. Level up, accessorize, and choose from a huge catalogue of melee weapons on your quest to fight your way through the hordes of monsters and powerful bosses awaiting you in the darkest depths of the isle.

Rockstar Games has announced GTA 6: The Festival, a special live music event being held to celebrate the launch of the open world action game.

Presented in partnership with PlayStation, the one-off event takes place in Miami, Florida on 21st November, 2026.

"Experience the music of Vice City and Leonida at Grand Theft Auto VI: The Festival, a special live event on November 21 at Mana Wynwood in Miami to celebrate the launch of GTAVI, featuring a star-studded lineup of artists from the game and the official soundtrack."

They will play "nonstop music" across two stages.

EXCLUSIVE: Carrie-Anne Moss (The Matrix) has joined the cast of Halsey and Avan Jogia’s psycho-sexual horror Replacer.

The Montreal-set horror stars Halsey as troubled DJ Proxy, who meets an alluring artist and his group of friends who run an underground radio station.

The station’s broadcast becomes warped by a mysterious signal hidden deep beneath the city’s subway system. Proxy must escape before the sound turns her, and her new friends, into something raw, primal, and unrecognizable.

The picture, written by Halsey and directed by Jogia, recently wrapped production in Montreal. Producer and composer Boys Noize (Alex Ridha) will score the film. Sky: Ramos involved but has minimal support – the numbers that illustrate Amorim’s point

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Some interesting analysis on AC Milan and Goncalo Ramos’ attacking struggles has been provided by the Sky studios.

It was a familiar tale for Ramos during Milan’s 2-1 win over Genoa on Sunday. He was involved but rarely inside the box, then he was selfless instead of ruthless in front of goal when he chose to square for Christian Pulisic to make it 2-0.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

The worries are starting to grow regarding whether the Portuguese can provide the output required of a number nine that cost €70m. Yet, the Rossoneri have scored 23 goals in 11 league games which suggests goals are coming from elsewhere, sometimes with Ramos’ help.

Sky offered some interesting insight after Sunday’s game. Firstly, they picked out just how involved Ramos actually is with various aspects of Milan’s overall play.

For example, he is first in the Milan squad in Serie A for interceptions and recoveries, while he is second for receptions, touches and duels. He is third for passes.

He is first among Rossoneri players for touches in the middle third of the field and touches in the opposition half. Looking league-wide, he is second for fouls committed plus fouls suffered among forwards, with 41.

Sky then shift the focus to the team’s overall play to try and explain why Ramos perhaps isn’t getting the quality service and is having to drop deeper as a result.

They look at data per 30 minutes of possession. Milan are first in Serie A for build-up actions, but they are 20th for entries into the final third and entries into the opposition box.

Amorim’s side are 19th for percentage of touches in the attacking third and 18th in percentage of touches in the opposition box. With numbers like these, there isn’t much for Ramos to get on the end of.

What does this tell us? Well, Amorim’s post match assessment about struggling to get the ball from the defensive third into the final third are true. Milan have a lot of the ball but still struggle to progress it, as the coach told Sky.

“I think it’s difficult for a striker when he doesn’t score goals. But I’ll repeat: we don’t spend much time in the final third of the pitch. We have possession, we’re improving our ball output, but we’re missing that. When we’re consistently in the final third, Gonçalo will score a lot of goals,” he said.

“It’s a team problem, we’re not helping Ramos, but he’s helping us a lot. It’s a process, a journey. And we’re not yet at the point of being so dominant that Ramos spends most of his time in the opponent’s penalty area. We’re still a long way off.”

Jamal Musiala, serious illness

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The nightmare season that Bayern Munich is going through in terms of the infirmary is not limited to traditional physical injuries, even if the case of Manuel Neuer is worrying to say the least. While Vincent Kompany had to observe forced rest following a burn-out, it is the extra-sporting situation of Jamal Musiala which is polarizing all the discussions in Bavaria. Rarely seen on the pitch – only four appearances and just over 200 minutes of play this season – the 23-year-old attacking midfielder is affected by a health problem of rare complexity for an athlete of this level.

This summer, the German prodigy suffered two successive illnesses in the middle of a match during summer preparation meetings. Forcing the club to temporarily exclude him from major events, such as the clash against Dortmund in the German Super Cup, these episodes pushed Musiala to speak out publicly in mid-August.

He then mentioned a “neurological dysfunction” characterized by “brief and temporary absences, easily treatable”ensuring that there was no additional vital risk. But since then, added to this neurological disorder has been a tear in the muscle fibers contracted at the end of September in selection, keeping him away from the field for a long time.

It was on German television and on Bayerischer Rundfunk radio that Uli Hoeness threw a wrench into the pond, revealing the depths of the Bavarian leaders’ thoughts. Without filter, the honorary president exposed all the complexity of the file: “We are faced with a dilemma with him: he suffers from a disease that is difficult to cure, but which can be controlled to a certain extent with drug treatment. However, we don’t know if it will ever return to its former levels. »

A speech of immense transparency, which casts an icy chill on the sporting future of the German international. However, despite the doubt that hangs over his ability to become the flamboyant player he was again, Hoeness wanted to close the debate on the contractual level. Recently extended in gold until 2030 with emoluments around 25 million euros per year, the midfielder will not be let go by his institution: “He is still receiving his salary, but now imagine if Bayern questioned his contract saying: ‘The current performance level is not that of his contract.’ »

Barcelona are reportedly preparing to strengthen their defensive options during the January transfer window, with Arsenal defender Piero Hincapie emerging as one of the players on their radar.

According to a Sports Illustrated report, the Spanish giants are determined to bring in a left-footed centre-back as Hansi Flick looks to address defensive shortcomings in his squad.

The Barcelona manager has had to deal with several defensive injuries this season, and the club is now exploring potential reinforcements ahead of the winter transfer window.

Inter Milan’s Alessandro Bastoni and Manchester City’s Josko Gvardiol have reportedly been considered, while Manchester United defender Lisandro Martinez is another name on Barcelona’s shortlist.

However, Arsenal’s Hincapie is also attracting attention from the Catalan outfit. The Ecuadorian international’s ability to play both centre-back and left-back makes him an attractive option for Flick.

A move could prove difficult, though, with Fabrizio Romano casting serious doubt on Arsenal allowing the defender to leave midway through the season.

Discussing speculation surrounding a possible swap deal involving Hincapie and Alejandro Balde, Romano said: “Arsenal have no intention to sell Hincapie.”

He added that the Gunners are extremely satisfied with their defensive options and have no plans to change their backline in January.

Romano also highlighted the versatile defender’s importance to Mikel Arteta, describing his ability to play multiple defensive positions as a major asset.

Barcelona’s interest in Hincapie is understandable, considering their need for greater depth at the back. However, convincing Arsenal to negotiate could be a significant challenge.

The Gunners consider the 24-year-old an important part of their defensive setup, and it remains to be seen whether Barcelona will pursue alternative targets instead.

With several defensive options under consideration, Flick’s side will need to identify a realistic signing if it wants to address its defensive concerns in January.

86 Trillion: Shiba Inu (SHIB) Just Crossed the Line

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Shiba Inu has crossed the bullish threshold: the 85 trillion level is gone, and the available supply on exchanges has now reached 86 trillion, which puts even more pressure on an already oversold and exhausted asset that has been struggling to find a base for recovery.

Mean exchange inflow data shows a 606% increase on the 7-day moving average. All exchange-inflow related metrics are in green, which suggests a substantial injection of funds on the investors' side. Shiba Inu has been struggling on the market since the beginning of October and increased pressure isn't something we'd want to see.

Overall netflow stays positive, at 42 billion SHIB tokens in 24 hours. The dynamic is clearly bearish and reflects a consistent, gradual selling that is unlikely to make things better for the asset in the near term.

For those unfamiliar with on-chain dynamics in general: positive netflows suggest the presence of liquidity flowing into exchanges, creating a constant, strong source of pressure on the price. Even if not sold immediately, supply sitting on exchanges is usually destined to be sold at some point in time.

Historically, Shiba Inu has had a large share of its supply sitting on centralized exchanges, and almost every market rally it had since 2023 was followed by a surge of both inflows and sell-side volumes.

Shiba Inu's technical performance on the market isn't better than its on-chain counterpart. The asset has been struggling since the beginning of October, as mentioned above. The price lost more than 28% of its value and is set for a further loss potentially.

With rising inflows on top of it, we get a recipe for disaster: negative sentiment, a constant source of selling pressure, a worrying general market picture, and a series of hacks, exploits and other illicit activities surrounding the industry in general.

Momentum indicators are also 'in red': RSI is moving sideways around the reading of 30, hinting at the current trend continuation.

The trading volume is somewhat flat, suggesting constant pressure, and the liquidity clusters sit at levels the price has not reached yet.
